<a id='question-071'></a>

### Question 071: Prerenal Azotemia and the BUN/Creatinine Ratio
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Prerenal azotemia causes a disproportionate increase in BUN relative to creatinine due to increased proximal tubular reabsorption of urea secondary to elevated peritubular capillary oncotic pressure resulting from a high filtration fraction. (肾前性氮质血症导致BUN相对于肌酐不成比例的升高，这是由于高滤过分数导致高足管周围毛细血管胶体渗透压，从而驱动近端小管对尿素的大量重吸收的结果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 75-year-old female presents to the emergency department after experiencing severe gastroenteritis with intractable vomiting for the past 3 days. She reports feeling dizzy and weak. On examination, her blood pressure is 85/55 mm Hg, heart rate is 115 bpm, and she has dry mucous membranes and poor skin turgor. Laboratory results show: Serum BUN 56 mg/dL, Serum Creatinine 2.0 mg/dL, Serum Sodium 138 mEq/L, Serum Potassium 4.2 mEq/L, Serum Albumin 3.5 g/dL, Urinalysis shows specific gravity 1.025, and Fractional Excretion of Sodium (FeNa) is 0.4%. The BUN/Creatinine ratio is 28:1. The physician explains that the elevated BUN/Creatinine ratio is characteristic of her condition. Which of the following physiological mechanisms best explains why the serum BUN-to-creatinine ratio is disproportionately elevated in this patient?

(A) Decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR) leads to reduced clearance of both BUN and creatinine, but BUN is cleared more rapidly than creatinine, causing the ratio to decrease.
(B) Increased tubular secretion of creatinine in the setting of hypovolemia leads to a higher serum creatinine level, thus decreasing the BUN/Creatinine ratio.
(C) The high filtration fraction associated with hypovolemia increases water reabsorption in the proximal tubule, leading to increased passive reabsorption of urea, while creatinine is not significantly reabsorbed.
(D) Decreased renal plasma flow (RPF) leads to increased hydrostatic pressure in the peritubular capillaries, promoting filtration of plasma proteins and increasing the peritubular oncotic pressure, which enhances urea reabsorption.
(E) Increased protein intake during the gastroenteritis episode leads to increased BUN production, while creatinine production remains constant, thus increasing the BUN/Creatinine ratio.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 75-year-old female with severe gastroenteritis, intractable vomiting (3 days), dizziness, weakness, hypotension (85/55 mmHg), tachycardia (115 bpm), signs of dehydration (dry mucous membranes, poor skin turgor). This points towards significant volume depletion (hypovolemia).
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   BUN = 56 mg/dL, Creatinine = 2.0 mg/dL. BUN/Creatinine ratio = 56/2.0 = 28:1. This ratio is significantly elevated (>20:1), which is a classic finding in prerenal azotemia.
    *   FeNa = 0.4%. This is low (<1%), indicating avid sodium retention by the kidneys, consistent with hypovolemia and activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS).
    *   Urine specific gravity = 1.025. This is relatively high, indicating concentrated urine, consistent with the kidneys attempting to conserve water in response to dehydration.
    *   Serum Albumin = 3.5 g/dL (normal). This rules out hypoalbuminemia as a cause of decreased oncotic pressure.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the physiological mechanism explaining the disproportionately elevated BUN/Creatinine ratio in this context (prerenal azotemia due to hypovolemia).

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
*   **Prerenal Azotemia Pathophysiology:** Hypovolemia (due to vomiting/diarrhea) leads to decreased effective circulating volume. This triggers compensatory mechanisms:
    *   **Sympathetic Nervous System Activation:** Causes afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, reducing RPF and GFR.
    *   **RAAS Activation:** Angiotensin II causes preferential efferent arteriolar vasoconstriction (more potent effect than on afferent arterioles).
*   **Hemodynamic Effects:** The net effect of sympathetic activation and Angiotensin II is a significant decrease in RPF, but GFR is partially preserved due to efferent arteriolar constriction maintaining glomerular hydrostatic pressure ($P_{GC}$).
*   **Filtration Fraction (FF):** FF = GFR / RPF. Since RPF decreases more dramatically than GFR in prerenal azotemia, the FF increases significantly (often > 0.30 or 30%).
*   **Glomerular Filtration:** A high FF means a larger proportion of plasma volume is filtered per unit time. This leads to increased concentration of solutes in the glomerular filtrate, including proteins like albumin.
*   **Peritubular Capillary Oncotic Pressure ($\pi_c$):** The increased filtration of proteins (especially albumin) leads to a higher concentration of proteins in the peritubular capillaries. Since the volume of fluid entering the peritubular capillaries is also increased (due to high FF), the oncotic pressure ($\pi_c$) in these capillaries rises significantly (often > 40 mmHg).
*   **Proximal Tubule Reabsorption:** The high $\pi_c$ creates a strong osmotic gradient that pulls water from the tubular lumen into the peritubular capillaries. This massive water reabsorption occurs primarily in the proximal tubule.
*   **Urea Reabsorption:** Urea is passively reabsorbed in the proximal tubule along with water, driven by the water reabsorption gradient. Normally, about 50% of filtered urea is reabsorbed. However, with maximal water reabsorption driven by high $\pi_c$, urea reabsorption increases dramatically (often > 75%).
*   **Creatinine Handling:** Creatinine is freely filtered at the glomerulus, minimally secreted by the tubules, and essentially not reabsorbed. Its clearance is primarily determined by GFR.
*   **BUN/Creatinine Ratio:** Because urea reabsorption is markedly increased while creatinine reabsorption is negligible, the serum BUN level rises disproportionately more than the serum creatinine level during prerenal azotemia. This results in a high BUN/Creatinine ratio (>20:1).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR) leads to reduced clearance of both BUN and creatinine, but BUN is cleared more rapidly than creatinine, causing the ratio to decrease.** This is incorrect. While GFR decreases in prerenal azotemia, the *relative* decrease in GFR is less than the decrease in RPF, leading to an *increased* FF. Furthermore, the *reason* for the elevated ratio is not differential clearance but differential tubular reabsorption. BUN clearance is indeed faster than creatinine clearance under normal conditions, but this doesn't explain the *increase* in the ratio seen in prerenal states.
*   **(B) Increased tubular secretion of creatinine in the setting of hypovolemia leads to a higher serum creatinine level, thus decreasing the BUN/Creatinine ratio.** This is incorrect. Tubular secretion of creatinine is generally minor and does not significantly change in prerenal azotemia. Hypovolemia primarily affects GFR and RPF, not creatinine secretion. Increased secretion would *lower* serum creatinine, not raise it, and would decrease the ratio.
*   **(D) Decreased renal plasma flow (RPF) leads to increased hydrostatic pressure in the peritubular capillaries, promoting filtration of plasma proteins and increasing the peritubular oncotic pressure, which enhances urea reabsorption.** This is incorrect. Decreased RPF leads to *decreased* hydrostatic pressure in the peritubular capillaries, not increased. The increased peritubular oncotic pressure is due to increased filtration of proteins (driven by high FF), not increased hydrostatic pressure.
*   **(E) Increased protein intake during the gastroenteritis episode leads to increased BUN production, while creatinine production remains constant, thus increasing the BUN/Creatinine ratio.** This is incorrect. While increased protein intake can raise BUN, it is not the primary mechanism in this acute prerenal state caused by vomiting and diarrhea. The patient is likely experiencing catabolism and decreased intake, not increased protein intake. Moreover, the hallmark of prerenal azotemia is the disproportionate rise in BUN *relative to creatinine* due to altered hemodynamics and tubular reabsorption, not simply increased production.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prerenal azotemia is characterized by hypovolemia leading to a high filtration fraction, increased peritubular capillary oncotic pressure, and subsequent enhanced proximal tubular reabsorption of urea, resulting in a BUN/Creatinine ratio > 20:1. This contrasts with intrinsic renal failure (e.g., acute tubular necrosis) where tubular function is impaired, leading to decreased urea reabsorption and a lower BUN/Creatinine ratio (often <15:1), despite potentially lower GFR. Postrenal azotemia (obstruction) can have variable ratios depending on the chronicity and degree of obstruction.

<a id='question-072'></a>

### Question 072: Preeclampsia Pathophysiology: Glomerular Endotheliosis and Anti-angiogenic Factors
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Preeclampsia involves placental ischemia leading to the release of anti-angiogenic factors (sFlt-1, sEng) into the maternal circulation, which neutralize VEGF, causing glomerular endotheliosis, reduced glomerular filtration coefficient ($K_f$), decreased GFR, and proteinuria. (妊娠期高血压综合征涉及胎盘缺血导致抗血管生成因子（sFlt-1，sEng）释放到母体循环中，中和VEGF，引起肾小球内皮细胞病变，降低滤过系数Kf，降低GFR，并导致蛋白尿。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old primigravida at 34 weeks gestation presents to the emergency department complaining of severe headache and blurred vision. Her blood pressure is 165/105 mmHg, and she has significant facial and lower extremity edema. Urinalysis reveals 3+ protein. A 24-hour urine collection confirms 3.5 g of protein excretion. Serum creatinine is 1.2 mg/dL (baseline 0.7 mg/dL), and BUN is 25 mg/dL. Renal biopsy performed after delivery shows marked swelling of glomerular endothelial cells, loss of fenestrations, and narrowing of the capillary lumens. Electron microscopy reveals cytoplasmic vacuolization within the swollen endothelial cells. This constellation of findings is characteristic of glomerular endotheliosis seen in preeclampsia. Which of the following placental-derived factors is primarily responsible for neutralizing circulating Vascular Endothelial Growth Factor (VEGF) and initiating this glomerular pathology?

(A) Tumor Necrosis Factor-alpha (TNF-α)
(B) Interleukin-6 (IL-6)
(C) Soluble fms-like tyrosine kinase 1 (sFlt-1)
(D) Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2)
(E) Endothelin-1 (ET-1)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old primigravida at 34 weeks gestation. This places her in the typical demographic and gestational age range for preeclampsia onset.
- **Symptoms:** Severe headache, blurred vision, facial/lower extremity edema. These are classic symptoms of severe preeclampsia, indicating end-organ dysfunction (CNS, renal).
- **Vital Signs:** BP 165/105 mmHg. This meets the criteria for severe hypertension in pregnancy (>160 systolic or >110 diastolic).
- **Laboratory Findings:** 3+ proteinuria (3.5 g/24h), elevated creatinine (1.2 mg/dL from baseline 0.7 mg/dL), elevated BUN (25 mg/dL). These indicate significant renal involvement, specifically glomerular damage leading to impaired filtration and proteinuria. The rise in creatinine indicates a decrease in GFR.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Marked swelling of glomerular endothelial cells, loss of fenestrations, narrowing of capillary lumens, cytoplasmic vacuolization. These are the hallmark features of glomerular endotheliosis, the characteristic renal lesion of preeclampsia.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *placental-derived factor* responsible for neutralizing VEGF and causing glomerular endotheliosis. This directs the focus to the specific pathophysiology linking placental dysfunction to glomerular injury.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Preeclampsia Pathogenesis:** Preeclampsia is thought to originate from defective trophoblast invasion of the maternal spiral arteries early in pregnancy. This leads to inadequate placental perfusion and ischemia.
- **Ischemic Placenta Response:** The ischemic placenta releases various factors into the maternal circulation, including anti-angiogenic factors.
- **Key Anti-angiogenic Factors:** The two primary anti-angiogenic factors implicated are:
    - **Soluble fms-like tyrosine kinase 1 (sFlt-1):** This is a soluble receptor for both VEGF and placental growth factor (PlGF). By binding to VEGF, sFlt-1 prevents VEGF from binding to its native receptors (VEGFR-1, VEGFR-2) on endothelial cells. This neutralization of VEGF impairs endothelial cell function, survival, and angiogenesis.
    - **Soluble endoglin (sEng):** Endoglin is a co-receptor for TGF-β ligands, which are involved in angiogenesis and vascular remodeling. sEng binds to endoglin and inhibits TGF-β signaling, contributing to endothelial dysfunction and hypertension.
- **VEGF's Role in Glomerulus:** VEGF is crucial for maintaining the integrity and function of the glomerular endothelium. It promotes endothelial cell survival, maintains fenestrations, and regulates permeability. Podocytes also produce VEGF.
- **Glomerular Endotheliosis Mechanism:** In preeclampsia, the elevated levels of sFlt-1 (and sEng) neutralize circulating VEGF. This VEGF deficiency leads to:
    - **Endothelial Cell Swelling:** Glomerular endothelial cells become swollen and develop cytoplasmic vacuoles.
    - **Fenestration Loss:** The characteristic fenestrae (pores) in the endothelial cells are lost.
    - **Capillary Lumen Occlusion:** The swelling and potential detachment of endothelial cells can narrow or occlude the glomerular capillary lumens.
    - **Reduced Kf:** The loss of fenestrations and potential narrowing of the capillary lumen significantly reduces the glomerular filtration coefficient ($K_f$), which is proportional to the surface area and permeability of the filtration barrier. ($K_f = Lp_m * A$, where Lp_m is hydraulic permeability and A is filtration surface area).
    - **Decreased GFR:** The reduction in $K_f$ leads to a decrease in the glomerular filtration rate (GFR), contributing to the rise in serum creatinine and BUN observed in the patient.
    - **Proteinuria:** Endothelial dysfunction and damage to the filtration barrier increase its permeability to proteins, leading to proteinuria.
- **Summary:** The placental ischemia in preeclampsia leads to the release of sFlt-1, which neutralizes VEGF. This VEGF deficiency causes glomerular endothelial cell injury (endotheliosis), loss of fenestrations, reduced $K_f$, decreased GFR, and proteinuria.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tumor Necrosis Factor-alpha (TNF-α):** TNF-α is a pro-inflammatory cytokine. While inflammation plays a role in preeclampsia, TNF-α is not the primary factor responsible for neutralizing VEGF and causing glomerular endotheliosis. Elevated TNF-α levels have been observed in preeclampsia, but its direct role in VEGF neutralization is not established.
- (B) **Interleukin-6 (IL-6):** IL-6 is another pro-inflammatory cytokine. Similar to TNF-α, elevated IL-6 levels are found in preeclampsia, but it does not directly neutralize VEGF or cause the specific endothelial changes seen in glomerular endotheliosis.
- (D) **Prostaglandin E2 (PGE2):** PGE2 is involved in vasodilation and renal blood flow regulation. While renal vasoconstriction contributes to hypertension in preeclampsia, PGE2 is not the primary mediator of glomerular endotheliosis or VEGF neutralization. In fact, some studies suggest decreased PGE2 production might contribute to vasoconstriction.
- (E) **Endothelin-1 (ET-1):** ET-1 is a potent vasoconstrictor. Elevated levels of ET-1 are found in preeclampsia and contribute significantly to the hypertension and renal vasoconstriction. However, ET-1 does not directly neutralize VEGF or cause the characteristic endothelial swelling and fenestration loss of glomerular endotheliosis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Preeclampsia is characterized by placental ischemia leading to the release of anti-angiogenic factors, primarily sFlt-1, which neutralizes VEGF, causing glomerular endotheliosis, reduced GFR, and proteinuria. This mechanism distinguishes preeclampsia from other causes of proteinuria and hypertension in pregnancy, such as chronic hypertension or gestational hypertension, which do not typically involve this specific placental-derived anti-angiogenic pathway leading to glomerular endotheliosis.

---

<a id='question-073'></a>

### Question 073: Intravascular Hemolysis and Hemoglobinuria
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Massive intravascular hemolysis leads to the release of free hemoglobin into the plasma. When plasma haptoglobin is saturated, hemoglobin dissociates into dimers, which are small enough to be filtered by the glomerulus, leading to hemoglobinuria and potential acute kidney injury (Pigment Cast Nephropathy). (大量血管内溶血导致游离血红蛋白释放，当血浆启动蛋白饱和时，血红蛋白解离成二聚体，小到足以被肾小球滤过，导致血红蛋白尿和潜在的急性肾损伤（色素铸型肾病）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department 2 hours after receiving an incompatible ABO blood transfusion. He reports acute onset of high fever (39.5°C), severe flank pain, and chills. His urine is described as dark burgundy-colored. Vital signs show: BP 90/60 mmHg, HR 120 bpm, RR 24 breaths/min. Physical examination reveals pallor and mild jaundice. Laboratory results show: Serum creatinine 2.5 mg/dL (baseline 0.8 mg/dL), BUN 45 mg/dL, Serum LDH 2500 U/L (normal <250 U/L), Serum bilirubin (total) 4.0 mg/dL (normal <1.2 mg/dL), Serum haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (normal 30-200 mg/dL), Urinalysis: 4+ blood (dipstick), microscopic examination shows 1-2 RBCs/hpf, no casts initially. Given the clinical presentation and laboratory findings consistent with acute intravascular hemolysis, which of the following best explains why free hemoglobin appears in the urine despite the low number of red blood cells seen on microscopy?

(A) Red blood cells lyse within the renal tubules, releasing hemoglobin directly into the tubular lumen.
(B) The glomerular filtration barrier is damaged by the inflammatory response to the transfusion reaction, allowing intact red blood cells to pass into Bowman's space.
(C) Hemoglobin tetramers are large enough to be retained by the glomerular filtration barrier, but smaller heme molecules are filtered.
(D) Free hemoglobin in the plasma dissociates into smaller alpha-beta dimers upon haptoglobin saturation, allowing these dimers to pass through the glomerular filtration barrier.
(E) The high concentration of free heme released during hemolysis directly damages the glomerular basement membrane, increasing its permeability to plasma proteins, including albumin and hemoglobin.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male, incompatible ABO transfusion, acute fever, back pain, dark burgundy urine, oliguria. This strongly suggests acute intravascular hemolysis secondary to a transfusion reaction.
- **Vital Signs:** Hypotension (BP 90/60 mmHg) and tachycardia (HR 120 bpm) indicate potential hypovolemia or shock.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Elevated LDH and bilirubin: Indicate red blood cell destruction (hemolysis).
    - Low haptoglobin: Haptoglobin binds free hemoglobin; its depletion confirms significant intravascular hemolysis.
    - Elevated creatinine and BUN: Indicate acute kidney injury (AKI).
    - Urinalysis: 4+ blood on dipstick suggests significant heme pigment in the urine. However, microscopic examination shows only 1-2 RBCs/hpf. This discrepancy is the key clue. The dipstick detects heme (from hemoglobin or myoglobin), not just intact RBCs. The low RBC count indicates that the red color is not primarily due to intact red blood cells passing through the glomerulus.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the presence of hemoglobin in the urine despite the low RBC count.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Intravascular Hemolysis:** RBC destruction within blood vessels releases hemoglobin into the plasma.
- **Hemoglobin Structure:** Hemoglobin exists primarily as a tetramer (α2β2) in intact RBCs. In plasma, it dissociates into alpha-beta dimers (αβ, MW ~32 kDa, molecular radius ~2.5 nm).
- **Haptoglobin:** A plasma protein that binds free hemoglobin dimers, forming a complex that is cleared by the reticuloendothelial system (primarily in the liver). This prevents free hemoglobin from being filtered by the glomerulus and causing kidney damage.
- **Glomerular Filtration Barrier:** Composed of the fenestrated endothelium of glomerular capillaries, the glomerular basement membrane (GBM), and the podocytes with their slit diaphragms. This barrier restricts the passage of large molecules (like albumin, MW ~66 kDa) and cells (like RBCs). Molecules smaller than albumin (e.g., glucose, amino acids, ions, small peptides, and hemoglobin dimers) are freely filtered.
- **Mechanism in Hemolysis:** During massive intravascular hemolysis, the amount of free hemoglobin released overwhelms the plasma haptoglobin. Once haptoglobin is saturated, free hemoglobin dimers remain in the plasma. These dimers (MW ~32 kDa, radius ~2.5 nm) are small enough to pass freely through the glomerular filtration barrier into Bowman's space.
- **Kidney Damage (Pigment Cast Nephropathy):** Filtered hemoglobin is reabsorbed and metabolized by proximal tubular cells, leading to hemosiderin deposition (hemosiderinuria). However, excessive filtered hemoglobin can overwhelm tubular reabsorptive capacity. In the distal tubule, hemoglobin can precipitate with Tamm-Horsfall mucoprotein, forming pigmented casts that obstruct tubules, leading to acute tubular necrosis (ATN) and AKI. Free heme iron can also generate reactive oxygen species, causing direct tubular cell injury and intense intrarenal vasoconstriction.
- **Therefore:** The presence of hemoglobinuria in the absence of significant hematuria (intact RBCs in urine) is explained by the filtration of free hemoglobin dimers after haptoglobin saturation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While some RBC lysis can occur within tubules, the primary mechanism for hemoglobinuria in massive intravascular hemolysis is glomerular filtration of free hemoglobin dimers, not tubular lysis. The low RBC count on microscopy argues against significant tubular lysis contributing to the urine color.
- (B): **Incorrect.** The glomerular filtration barrier is generally intact in this scenario. While severe inflammation can potentially cause damage, the primary reason for hemoglobinuria is the size of the hemoglobin dimer, not a generalized increase in glomerular permeability allowing intact RBCs. Intact RBCs are much larger (7-8 μm diameter) and are normally retained by the barrier.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Hemoglobin is filtered as a dimer, not as a tetramer. The tetramer (MW ~64.5 kDa) is too large to be freely filtered. Heme itself is a smaller molecule, but it's released from hemoglobin and is not the primary filtered species responsible for the characteristic hemoglobinuria. The key is the dissociation into *dimers*.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While free heme can cause oxidative damage, the primary mechanism for hemoglobinuria is the filtration of hemoglobin *dimers* due to their size relative to the glomerular filtration barrier, especially after haptoglobin depletion. Direct damage to the GBM by heme is not the main reason for the filtration of hemoglobin itself. The barrier's permeability to albumin is not typically affected in this acute setting.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Understand the role of haptoglobin in preventing hemoglobinuria and the size-dependent filtration of hemoglobin dimers across the glomerulus during massive intravascular hemolysis. Recognize the discrepancy between dipstick "blood" and microscopic RBC count as a hallmark of hemoglobinuria.
- **Differentials:** Hemoglobinuria (due to intravascular hemolysis, transfusion reaction, burns, etc.) vs. Myoglobinuria (due to rhabdomyolysis) vs. Hematuria (due to glomerular disease, stones, infection, trauma). All three can cause positive dipstick blood, but microscopy differentiates them. Hemoglobinuria typically has few RBCs, myoglobinuria has few RBC casts, and hematuria has numerous RBCs (and potentially RBC casts).

<a id='question-074'></a>

### Question 074: Rhabdomyolysis and Acute Kidney Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Rhabdomyolysis causes the release of myoglobin, which is freely filtered by the glomerulus due to its low molecular weight and monomeric structure, leading to acute tubular necrosis (pigment nephropathy). (肌溶解症导致肌红蛋白释放，由于其低分子量和单体结构，被肾小球自由滤过，导致急性肾小管坏死（色素性肾病）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
An 18-year-old male military recruit presents to the emergency department after collapsing during a strenuous 10-mile march in extreme heat (100°F). He reports severe, diffuse muscle pain and stiffness that began shortly after the march. His urine is described as dark, tea-colored. Vital signs show a heart rate of 110 bpm, blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and temperature of 101.5°F. Physical examination reveals generalized muscle tenderness. Laboratory results show a serum creatine kinase (CK) level of 85,000 U/L (normal < 200 U/L), serum creatinine of 4.5 mg/dL (normal 0.7-1.3 mg/dL), BUN of 40 mg/dL (normal 7-20 mg/dL), serum potassium of 6.0 mEq/L (normal 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), and serum calcium of 7.5 mg/dL (normal 8.5-10.5 mg/dL). Urinalysis shows a strongly positive result for blood on dipstick, but microscopic examination reveals no red blood cells (RBCs). The urine sediment shows granular casts and muddy brown casts.

Which physiological property of myoglobin allows it to enter tubular lumens and cause acute pigment nephropathy in rhabdomyolysis?

(A) Its high molecular weight (> 69 kDa) prevents its reabsorption in the proximal tubule, leading to increased intratubular concentration and toxicity.
(B) Its ability to bind heme iron allows it to form complexes with Tamm-Horsfall protein, leading to tubular obstruction.
(C) Its low molecular weight (< 70 kDa) and monomeric structure allow it to be freely filtered across the glomerular filtration barrier.
(D) Its high affinity for nitric oxide (NO) leads to systemic vasodilation and decreased renal perfusion pressure.
(E) Its high protein-binding capacity in the plasma leads to decreased free myoglobin concentration and reduced glomerular filtration.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Young male, military recruit, strenuous exercise in heat – classic setting for exertional rhabdomyolysis.
- **Symptoms:** Severe muscle pain, dark tea-colored urine – hallmark signs of rhabdomyolysis and myoglobinuria.
- **Vitals:** Tachycardia, hypotension, tachypnea, fever – suggest systemic inflammatory response and potential volume depletion/shock.
- **Labs:** Extremely elevated CK (85,000 U/L) confirms massive muscle breakdown. Elevated creatinine and BUN indicate acute kidney injury (AKI). Hyperkalemia and hypocalcemia are common electrolyte abnormalities in rhabdomyolysis due to muscle cell lysis.
- **Urinalysis:** Positive dipstick for blood, but zero RBCs on microscopy – this is the classic finding for myoglobinuria. The dipstick detects heme, which is present in both hemoglobin and myoglobin. Microscopic examination rules out hematuria. Granular and muddy brown casts indicate acute tubular necrosis (ATN).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the physiological property of myoglobin that allows it to cause pigment nephropathy (AKI).

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Rhabdomyolysis:** Breakdown of skeletal muscle cells releases intracellular contents, including myoglobin, potassium, phosphate, creatine kinase, and uric acid, into the bloodstream.
- **Myoglobin Structure:** Myoglobin is a monomeric protein (molecular weight ~17.8 kDa, radius ~1.9 nm). It contains a heme group, which binds oxygen.
- **Glomerular Filtration:** The glomerular filtration barrier consists of the fenestrated endothelium of the glomerular capillaries, the glomerular basement membrane (GBM), and the podocytes with their filtration slits. This barrier restricts the passage of large molecules (like albumin, MW ~69 kDa) and cells. Molecules smaller than ~70 kDa and those that are not negatively charged are generally freely filtered.
- **Myoglobin Filtration:** Due to its low molecular weight (~17.8 kDa) and monomeric structure, myoglobin is readily filtered by the glomerulus.
- **Mechanism of AKI in Myoglobinuria:**
    1.  **Filtration:** Myoglobin is freely filtered.
    2.  **Vasoconstriction:** Myoglobin scavenges nitric oxide (NO), a potent vasodilator produced by the endothelium, leading to afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction and decreased renal blood flow (RBF) and glomerular filtration rate (GFR). Myoglobin may also stimulate the release of endothelin, another vasoconstrictor.
    3.  **Direct Tubular Toxicity:** In the acidic environment of the distal tubule and collecting duct (urine pH often < 5.6), myoglobin dissociates, releasing free iron (Fe2+/Fe3+). This iron can catalyze the formation of highly reactive hydroxyl radicals (Fenton reaction), leading to oxidative damage and necrosis of tubular epithelial cells.
    4.  **Tubular Obstruction:** Myoglobin can precipitate with Tamm-Horsfall mucoprotein (uromodulin) in the distal tubules, forming casts that obstruct tubular lumens, contributing to ATN.
- **The question specifically asks about the property allowing myoglobin to *enter* tubular lumens.** This occurs because it is freely filtered across the glomerulus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Myoglobin's molecular weight is *low* (~17.8 kDa), well below the threshold for significant reabsorption in the proximal tubule (which typically reabsorbs proteins < 69 kDa). If it were high (> 69 kDa), it would *not* be filtered and thus wouldn't cause pigment nephropathy. This option incorrectly states a high molecular weight and implies reabsorption is the issue, not filtration.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While myoglobin *does* form complexes with Tamm-Horsfall protein leading to cast formation and tubular obstruction, this is a *consequence* of its filtration and presence in the tubular lumen, not the primary property allowing it to *enter* the lumen. The question asks about the property enabling entry, which is filtration.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Myoglobin's effect on NO leads to *renal vasoconstriction*, not systemic vasodilation. Systemic vasodilation would decrease blood pressure and potentially worsen renal perfusion, but it's not the primary mechanism of myoglobin's renal toxicity, nor is it the property allowing it to enter the tubules.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Myoglobin has *low* protein binding in plasma (mostly unbound). High protein binding would *decrease* the amount of free myoglobin available for filtration, thus reducing its nephrotoxic potential. This option incorrectly states high protein binding.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Rhabdomyolysis releases myoglobin, a small, monomeric protein freely filtered by the glomerulus. This filtration, coupled with its potential for direct tubular toxicity and cast formation, leads to acute tubular necrosis (pigment nephropathy). Key differentials for AKI with dark urine include hemolysis (hemoglobinuria) and contrast-induced nephropathy. Hemoglobinuria also causes a positive dipstick for blood but lacks RBCs; however, hemoglobin is larger than myoglobin (~64.5 kDa) and is less readily filtered, though still filtered to some extent. Contrast agents are much larger and cause AKI primarily through direct tubular toxicity and vasoconstriction, not filtration.

<a id='question-075'></a>

### Question 075: Multiple Myeloma Cast Nephropathy: Bence Jones Proteinuria
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The mechanism underlying the failure of standard urine dipstick tests to detect monoclonal free light chains (Bence Jones proteins) in patients with multiple myeloma cast nephropathy, highlighting the specificity of the dipstick test for albumin. (标准尿试纸条无法检测多发性骨髓瘤患者中游离轻链蛋白的原因，强调试纸条只对白蛋白具有特异性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of chronic low back pain presents to the emergency department with worsening fatigue, bone pain, and confusion. His vital signs show a heart rate of 95 bpm, blood pressure of 130/80 mmHg, respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min, and temperature of 37.0°C. Physical examination reveals pallor and tenderness over the lumbar spine. Laboratory investigations reveal normocytic anemia (hemoglobin 9.0 g/dL), hypercalcemia (serum calcium 11.5 mg/dL), elevated serum creatinine (4.5 mg/dL), and elevated BUN (45 mg/dL). A routine urine dipstick analysis is negative for protein. However, a subsequent 24-hour urine collection shows significant proteinuria (4.0 g/day), and urine protein electrophoresis reveals a monoclonal kappa light chain spike. Renal biopsy shows dilated tubules containing eosinophilic, fractured casts surrounded by multinucleated giant cells.

What is the primary reason the initial urine dipstick test was negative for protein despite the patient having significant proteinuria confirmed by 24-hour urine collection and electrophoresis?

(A) The patient's hypercalcemia causes vasoconstriction of the afferent arteriole, leading to decreased GFR and reduced filtration of all proteins, including albumin.
(B) The monoclonal light chains are too large to be filtered by the glomerulus, leading to their accumulation in the serum and subsequent renal damage through other mechanisms.
(C) Standard urine dipsticks rely on a colorimetric reaction that specifically binds to the anionic nature of albumin at physiological urine pH, failing to detect the neutral or cationic monoclonal free light chains.
(D) The patient's acute kidney injury is primarily due to hypercalcemia-induced interstitial nephritis, which does not typically involve significant proteinuria.
(E) The high concentration of Tamm-Horsfall protein in the urine competitively inhibits the binding of the dipstick reagent to albumin, leading to a false-negative result.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 68-year-old male with back pain, fatigue, bone pain, confusion (suggestive of hypercalcemia and renal failure).
- **Labs:** Normocytic anemia, hypercalcemia (11.5 mg/dL), elevated creatinine (4.5 mg/dL), elevated BUN (45 mg/dL) - consistent with renal failure and likely underlying malignancy (multiple myeloma).
- **Urine Dipstick:** Negative for protein.
- **24-hour Urine:** 4.0 g/day proteinuria.
- **Urine Protein Electrophoresis:** Monoclonal kappa light chain spike.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Dilated tubules with eosinophilic, fractured casts surrounded by multinucleated giant cells (characteristic of myeloma cast nephropathy).
- **Key Discrepancy:** Significant proteinuria (4.0 g/day) detected by quantitative methods but absent on the routine urine dipstick. The question asks for the reason for this discrepancy.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Urine Dipstick Principle:** Standard urine dipsticks use a chromogenic reaction based on the protein error of indicator dyes (e.g., tetrabromophenol blue). These dyes are anionic at physiological urine pH (typically 5.5-6.5). The anionic dye binds strongly to the positively charged sites on albumin molecules (which are also anionic but have positively charged patches at physiological pH). This binding causes a color change (usually yellow to green/blue), the intensity of which correlates with the albumin concentration.
- **Bence Jones Proteins:** These are monoclonal free immunoglobulin light chains (kappa or lambda) produced in excess by malignant plasma cells in multiple myeloma. They are much smaller than intact immunoglobulins (MW ~22-44 kDa vs ~150 kDa).
- **Filtration:** Due to their small size, free light chains are readily filtered by the glomerulus.
- **Dipstick Specificity:** The dipstick reagent is highly specific for albumin. It does not bind effectively to other proteins, including free light chains, because free light chains lack the specific positively charged sites required for strong binding to the anionic dye, or they may be neutral or even cationic depending on the specific light chain and pH.
- **Conclusion:** The massive proteinuria in this patient is due to the filtration of monoclonal free light chains. Because standard dipsticks only detect albumin, they fail to register the presence of these free light chains, resulting in a false-negative test despite significant protein excretion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Hypercalcemia causes afferent arteriolar vasoconstriction, reducing RPF and GFR. While this contributes to the AKI, it would decrease the filtration of *all* proteins, including albumin, potentially leading to *less* proteinuria, not a discrepancy where albumin filtration is present but undetected. Furthermore, the dipstick test is designed to detect albumin, so reduced albumin filtration wouldn't explain a negative dipstick result in the face of known significant proteinuria.
- (B): Free light chains are small (22-44 kDa) and are readily filtered by the glomerulus. Their size is not the reason for dipstick negativity; their lack of binding affinity to the dipstick reagent is. If they weren't filtered, they wouldn't cause cast nephropathy.
- (D): Hypercalcemia can cause interstitial nephritis, but the classic presentation of multiple myeloma renal failure is cast nephropathy due to light chain precipitation. The biopsy findings (tubular casts) strongly support cast nephropathy, not primarily interstitial nephritis. Moreover, interstitial nephritis doesn't inherently explain the dipstick-proteinuria discrepancy.
- (E): Tamm-Horsfall protein (uromodulin) is involved in the *formation* of myeloma casts in the distal tubule/collecting duct, where it co-precipitates with light chains. It does not typically interfere with the dipstick assay for albumin in the initial urine sample. While very high concentrations of any protein could theoretically compete, this is not the primary mechanism for the dipstick's failure to detect light chains. The fundamental issue is the lack of specific binding between the dipstick reagent and light chains.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Standard urine dipsticks are specific for albumin and will not detect significant proteinuria caused by other proteins, such as monoclonal free light chains (Bence Jones proteins) in multiple myeloma. Quantitative urine protein measurement (e.g., 24-hour collection, urine protein/creatinine ratio) or specialized tests like sulfosalicylic acid precipitation or electrophoresis are needed to detect non-albumin proteinuria. Key differential diagnoses for dipstick-negative proteinuria include multiple myeloma, amyloidosis, and light chain deposition disease.

<a id='question-076'></a>

### Question 076: Tamm-Horsfall Glycoprotein Synthesis and Function
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identification of the specific nephron segment responsible for the synthesis of Tamm-Horsfall glycoprotein (uromodulin), its role as the structural matrix of urinary casts, and its physiological functions. (塔姆-霍斯福蛋白（尿模块蛋白）的合成部位、作为尿液铸型结构基质的作用及其生理功能)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 24-year-old male medical student is performing a routine urinalysis laboratory exercise. He examines a centrifuged urine sample under a microscope and observes several hyaline casts. He recalls that all urinary casts, regardless of their composition (hyaline, granular, cellular, etc.), share a common structural component. He also remembers that this protein is the most abundant protein found in normal human urine. He is tasked with identifying the specific segment of the nephron responsible for synthesizing this crucial protein. He knows the protein is heavily glycosylated, forms polymers, and plays a role in preventing urinary tract infections and kidney stone formation. Which nephron segment is the exclusive site of synthesis for this protein?

(A) Proximal Convoluted Tubule (PCT)
(B) Distal Convoluted Tubule (DCT)
(C) Collecting Duct (CD)
(D) Thick Ascending Limb (TAL) of the Loop of Henle
(E) Thin Descending Limb (TDL) of the Loop of Henle

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Observation of hyaline casts:** This points towards the presence of a protein matrix common to all casts.
- **"Common structural component" of all casts:** This directly refers to Tamm-Horsfall glycoprotein (uromodulin).
- **"Most abundant protein in normal human urine":** This is a key characteristic of uromodulin (~30-50 mg/day).
- **"Exclusive site of synthesis":** The question asks for the specific nephron segment responsible for producing this protein.
- **"Heavily glycosylated, forms polymers":** These are properties of uromodulin, consistent with its function as a mucin-like glycoprotein.
- **"Role in preventing UTIs and kidney stones":** These are known physiological functions of uromodulin.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
Tamm-Horsfall glycoprotein (THG), also known as uromodulin, is the most abundant protein in normal human urine. Its synthesis is restricted exclusively to the epithelial cells of the thick ascending limb (TAL) of the loop of Henle. THG is a large, heavily glycosylated protein anchored to the apical membrane of TAL cells via a glycosylphosphatidylinositol (GPI) anchor. It is secreted into the tubular lumen, where it polymerizes to form a gel-like matrix. This matrix serves several crucial physiological functions:
1.  **Structural Matrix of Casts:** THG forms the fundamental structural framework for all urinary casts, including hyaline casts, granular casts, red blood cell casts, white blood cell casts, and myeloma (Bence Jones protein) casts. The composition of the cast determines its appearance, but THG is always the underlying matrix.
2.  **Antimicrobial Defense:** The polymeric THG meshwork in the TAL lumen impedes the adhesion and colonization of uropathogenic bacteria, particularly *E. coli* expressing type 1 fimbriae, thereby providing innate immunity against urinary tract infections.
3.  **Inhibition of Crystallization:** THG inhibits the aggregation of calcium oxalate crystals, reducing the risk of nephrolithiasis (kidney stone formation).
4.  **Regulation of Ion Transport:** THG interacts with and modulates the activity of key ion transporters in the TAL, such as the Na+-K+-2Cl- cotransporter (NKCC2) and the renal outer medullary potassium channel (ROMK), contributing to the regulation of salt and water reabsorption.

The synthesis of THG is tightly regulated and is a hallmark feature of the TAL epithelium. Its presence in urine is a reliable marker for the integrity and function of this specific nephron segment.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Proximal Convoluted Tubule (PCT): The PCT is primarily involved in bulk reabsorption of water, electrolytes, glucose, and amino acids. It synthesizes proteins like megalin and cubilin (involved in endocytosis) and albumin (though most filtered albumin is reabsorbed, not synthesized here). It does *not* synthesize THG.
- (B) Distal Convoluted Tubule (DCT): The DCT is involved in fine-tuning electrolyte balance, particularly sodium and calcium reabsorption, under hormonal control (aldosterone, PTH). It does *not* synthesize THG.
- (C) Collecting Duct (CD): The CD is the final segment where water reabsorption (regulated by ADH) and urea transport occur. It also plays a role in acid-base balance. It does *not* synthesize THG.
- (E) Thin Descending Limb (TDL) of the Loop of Henle: The TDL is highly permeable to water but relatively impermeable to solutes. Its primary function is water reabsorption, contributing to the concentration of urine in the medulla. It does *not* synthesize THG.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Tamm-Horsfall glycoprotein (uromodulin) is exclusively synthesized by the thick ascending limb (TAL) of the loop of Henle and forms the structural matrix of all urinary casts. Its functions include antimicrobial defense and inhibition of crystal formation. Remember this protein's origin and role for urinalysis interpretation and understanding renal physiology. Differential diagnosis for urinary casts includes hyaline casts (normal or dehydration), granular casts (ATN), RBC casts (glomerulonephritis), WBC casts (pyelonephritis), and fatty casts (nephrotic syndrome).

<a id='question-077'></a>

### Question 077: Hypertensive Nephrosclerosis: Vascular Lesions and Renal Hemodynamics
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The characteristic histological vascular lesion (hyaline arteriolosclerosis) and its pathophysiological consequences (loss of autoregulation, increased intraglomerular pressure, secondary FSGS) in benign hypertensive nephrosclerosis. (良性高血压性肾硬化症的特征性组织学血管病变（透明质动脉硬化）及其病理生理后果（自调节能力丧失、球内压升高、继发性节段性硬化）)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a 25-year history of poorly controlled essential hypertension presents for evaluation of worsening fatigue and lower extremity edema. His blood pressure is 175/105 mmHg. Laboratory studies reveal a serum creatinine of 2.1 mg/dL (reference range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL) and a urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) of 400 mg/g (reference range <30 mg/g). Renal ultrasound shows symmetrically shrunken kidneys with a finely granular cortical surface. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals concentric, homogeneous, eosinophilic (pink) thickening of the walls of afferent arterioles, with luminal narrowing. There is no evidence of immune complex deposition on immunofluorescence microscopy, and electron microscopy shows no significant podocyte foot process effacement or subepithelial/subendothelial deposits. Which of the following histological vascular lesions is most characteristic of this patient's condition?

(A) Fibrinoid necrosis of afferent arterioles with crescent formation in Bowman's space.
(B) Necrotizing arteriolosclerosis with transmural fibrinoid necrosis and microaneurysm formation.
(C) Hyaline arteriolosclerosis with concentric thickening of afferent arteriolar walls.
(D) Immune complex deposition in the subepithelial space with "spike and dome" appearance on electron microscopy.
(E) Subendothelial deposits along the glomerular basement membrane with "wire loop" lesions on light microscopy.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with long-standing (25 years) poorly controlled essential hypertension. This history strongly suggests chronic hypertensive damage to the kidneys.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Worsening fatigue and lower extremity edema, elevated BP (175/105 mmHg), elevated serum creatinine (2.1 mg/dL), and mild proteinuria (UACR 400 mg/g). These findings indicate chronic kidney disease (CKD) likely secondary to hypertension.
*   **Imaging:** Symmetrically shrunken kidneys with granular cortical surface ('leather-grain appearance'). This gross pathology is characteristic of chronic ischemic damage, often seen in hypertensive nephrosclerosis.
*   **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    *   **Light Microscopy:** Concentric, homogeneous, eosinophilic (pink) thickening of afferent arteriolar walls with luminal narrowing. This is the classic description of hyaline arteriolosclerosis.
    *   **Immunofluorescence:** Negative for immune complex deposition, ruling out immune-mediated glomerulonephritis.
    *   **Electron Microscopy:** No significant podocyte effacement or deposits, further ruling out primary podocytopathies or immune complex diseases.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* histological vascular lesion.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
*   **Hypertension and Renal Microcirculation:** Chronic hypertension leads to increased shear stress on the endothelial cells lining the arterioles.
*   **Hyaline Arteriolosclerosis Pathogenesis:** This shear stress causes plasma proteins (like fibrinogen, albumin, and von Willebrand factor) to leak into the arteriolar wall. This triggers smooth muscle cell proliferation and increased synthesis of extracellular matrix components (collagen, fibronectin, proteoglycans) within the media and intima. The result is a concentric, homogeneous, eosinophilic (hyaline) thickening of the arteriolar wall, particularly affecting afferent arterioles.
*   **Consequences of Hyaline Arteriolosclerosis:**
    *   **Luminal Narrowing:** Reduces blood flow to the glomerulus, leading to glomerular ischemia and atrophy.
    *   **Loss of Autoregulation:** The thickened, rigid arterioles lose their ability to dilate and constrict appropriately in response to changes in systemic blood pressure. This impairs the kidney's ability to maintain a stable glomerular filtration rate (GFR) despite fluctuations in arterial pressure.
    *   **Increased Intraglomerular Pressure:** Because the afferent arteriole is less able to constrict, high systemic pressure is transmitted more directly into the glomerular capillaries, leading to increased intraglomerular hydrostatic pressure.
    *   **Secondary Glomerular Damage:** This sustained high intraglomerular pressure causes secondary damage to the glomeruli, often manifesting as focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), podocyte injury, and eventually glomerular sclerosis.
    *   **Tubulointerstitial Fibrosis:** Glomerular damage and ischemia lead to tubular atrophy and interstitial fibrosis, contributing to overall kidney dysfunction.
*   **Benign vs. Malignant Nephrosclerosis:** Benign nephrosclerosis (as described here) is characterized by hyaline arteriolosclerosis and gradual progression. Malignant nephrosclerosis involves more severe, rapidly progressive damage, often with fibrinoid necrosis of arterioles (necrotizing arteriolosclerosis) and microaneurysm formation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Fibrinoid necrosis of afferent arterioles with crescent formation in Bowman's space:** This describes malignant hypertension or rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN). Fibrinoid necrosis is characterized by deposition of fibrin and plasma proteins within the vessel wall, leading to vessel wall destruction. Crescent formation involves proliferation of parietal epithelial cells and infiltration of inflammatory cells into Bowman's space, typically seen in RPGN or severe glomerular injury. This is inconsistent with the patient's presentation of chronic, relatively mild proteinuria and the specific histological finding of hyaline thickening.
*   **(B) Necrotizing arteriolosclerosis with transmural fibrinoid necrosis and microaneurysm formation:** This is characteristic of *malignant* hypertension (malignant nephrosclerosis), a more severe and rapidly progressive form of hypertensive kidney disease. While related to hypertension, the histological lesion is distinct from the hyaline arteriolosclerosis seen in benign nephrosclerosis. The patient's presentation (chronic history, mild proteinuria, gradual decline) is more consistent with benign nephrosclerosis.
*   **(D) Immune complex deposition in the subepithelial space with "spike and dome" appearance on electron microscopy:** This describes membranous nephropathy, a primary glomerular disease caused by immune complex deposition. The "spike and dome" appearance refers to the subepithelial deposits (domes) separated by basement membrane material (spikes) seen on electron microscopy. This is ruled out by the negative immunofluorescence findings in the vignette.
*   **(E) Subendothelial deposits along the glomerular basement membrane with "wire loop" lesions on light microscopy:** This describes membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), often associated with immune complex deposition (e.g., Type I MPGN) or complement pathway dysregulation (e.g., Type II MPGN/Dense Deposit Disease). "Wire loop" lesions are thickened glomerular capillary walls due to subendothelial deposits. This is also ruled out by the negative immunofluorescence findings and the specific arteriolar lesion described.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hyaline arteriolosclerosis is the hallmark vascular lesion of benign hypertensive nephrosclerosis, resulting from chronic hypertension-induced damage to arteriolar walls. This leads to loss of autoregulation, increased intraglomerular pressure, and secondary glomerular damage (FSGS). Differentiate this from malignant nephrosclerosis (fibrinoid necrosis) and primary glomerular diseases (membranous nephropathy, MPGN) based on clinical presentation, renal function, proteinuria level, and specific histological findings (light microscopy, immunofluorescence, electron microscopy).

<a id='question-078'></a>

### Question 078: Malignant Hypertension and Renal Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Malignant hypertension causes characteristic vascular lesions in the kidney, including hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis (onion-skinning) and fibrinoid necrosis, leading to acute kidney injury and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia. (恶性高血压导致肾脏特征性血管病变，包括肥厚性小动脉硬化（洋葱皮样）和坏死性病变，导致急性肾损伤和微血管病性溶血性贫血。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old African American male presents to the emergency department complaining of a severe headache and blurred vision for the past 2 days. His past medical history is significant for poorly controlled essential hypertension, for which he takes no medications. On examination, his blood pressure is markedly elevated at 230/140 mm Hg. Funduscopic examination reveals papilledema. Laboratory studies show a serum creatinine of 4.8 mg/dL (baseline 1.2 mg/dL), BUN 45 mg/dL, and a peripheral blood smear shows numerous schistocytes. Urinalysis reveals mild proteinuria (1+ on dipstick) and microscopic hematuria. A renal biopsy is performed. Histopathological examination reveals concentric, laminated thickening of the walls of interlobular arterioles, resembling onion skin, along with areas of fibrinoid necrosis in afferent arterioles.

Which of the following histological findings is characteristic of hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis seen in malignant hypertension?

(A) Segmental sclerosis and hyalinosis of glomerular capillaries, associated with podocyte effacement.
(B) Immune complex deposition in the subepithelial space, forming "spikes" on silver stain.
(C) Concentric laminated proliferation of smooth muscle cells and basement membrane in interlobular arterioles.
(D) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane.
(E) Subendothelial electron-dense deposits with double contours of the glomerular basement membrane.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 42-year-old African American male with poorly controlled essential hypertension. This demographic and history increase the risk for severe hypertension and its complications.
- **Symptoms:** Severe headache, blurred vision, papilledema. These are classic signs of hypertensive encephalopathy and increased intracranial pressure, indicating end-organ damage from severely elevated blood pressure.
- **Vital Signs:** BP 230/140 mm Hg. This meets the criteria for malignant hypertension (severely elevated BP with acute end-organ damage).
- **Renal Function:** Acute kidney injury (Cr 4.8 mg/dL, BUN 45 mg/dL). This indicates acute renal damage secondary to the severe hypertension.
- **Hematology:** Schistocytes on peripheral smear. This indicates microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), caused by red blood cells being mechanically sheared as they pass through damaged, narrowed arterioles.
- **Urinalysis:** Mild proteinuria, microscopic hematuria. These are non-specific findings but consistent with glomerular injury or tubular damage from ischemia.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:** Concentric, laminated thickening of interlobular arterioles ("onion skin") and fibrinoid necrosis of afferent arterioles. These are the hallmark histological features of malignant (accelerated) hypertensive nephrosclerosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the characteristic histological finding of *hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis*.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Malignant Hypertension:** Defined as severely elevated blood pressure (typically >180/120 mmHg) associated with acute end-organ damage (e.g., papilledema, encephalopathy, acute renal failure, MAHA).
- **Pathophysiology:** Extremely high transmural pressure across arteriolar walls causes endothelial injury. This leads to plasma protein leakage, fibrin deposition, and platelet aggregation within the vessel wall (fibrinoid necrosis). Simultaneously, the high pressure stimulates proliferation of smooth muscle cells and increased synthesis of extracellular matrix (basement membrane), leading to concentric, laminated thickening of the arteriolar walls (hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis or "onion-skinning").
- **Renal Effects:** These vascular changes severely narrow the lumen of arterioles, particularly afferent arterioles, leading to glomerular ischemia and reduced GFR (acute kidney injury). The narrowing and damage also cause RBC fragmentation (MAHA). The "onion-skinning" specifically refers to the concentric proliferation of smooth muscle cells and deposition of basement membrane material in the walls of small arteries and arterioles, most prominently the interlobular arterioles in the kidney cortex. This process is driven by the sustained high pressure and associated growth factors.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Segmental sclerosis and hyalinosis of glomerular capillaries, associated with podocyte effacement. This describes changes seen in focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), a primary glomerular disease, or secondary FSGS (e.g., due to HIV, heroin use, obesity). While hypertension can worsen FSGS, these are not the primary lesions of malignant hypertension.
- (B) Immune complex deposition in the subepithelial space, forming "spikes" on silver stain. This describes membranous nephropathy, an immune-mediated glomerular disease characterized by subepithelial deposits (often IgG and C3) that cause thickening of the glomerular basement membrane and spike formation on silver stain. This is unrelated to malignant hypertension.
- (D) Linear deposition of IgG along the glomerular basement membrane. This is the characteristic finding in anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (Goodpasture syndrome), an autoimmune disease targeting type IV collagen in the GBM. This is unrelated to malignant hypertension.
- (E) Subendothelial electron-dense deposits with double contours of the glomerular basement membrane. This describes membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), often associated with immune complex deposition (e.g., C3 nephropathy, post-infectious GN, cryoglobulinemia). The "double contour" appearance results from mesangial cell interposition between the GBM and the subendothelial deposits. This is unrelated to malignant hypertension.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Malignant hypertension causes characteristic vascular lesions in the kidney: hyperplastic arteriolosclerosis ("onion-skinning") and fibrinoid necrosis. These lesions lead to severe glomerular ischemia, acute kidney injury, and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia. Differentiating these findings from other renal pathologies (glomerulonephritis, FSGS, vasculitis) is crucial. Remember that "onion-skinning" specifically refers to the concentric proliferation of smooth muscle and basement membrane in arterioles.

<a id='question-079'></a>

### Question 079: Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (STEC-HUS): Shiga Toxin and Endothelial Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Shiga toxin (Stx) produced by Shiga toxin-producing E. coli (STEC), such as E. coli O157:H7, binds to specific receptors on glomerular endothelial cells, initiating a cascade leading to endothelial damage, microthrombi formation, and thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) characteristic of Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS). (Shiga毒素结合肾小球内皮细胞受体，引发溶血尿毒综合征的微血栓性血管病)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 4-year-old girl presents to the emergency department 4 days after consuming an undercooked hamburger at a county fair. Initially, she experienced severe, bloody diarrhea. Over the past 2 days, she has become increasingly pale, lethargic, and developed petechiae on her trunk. Her mother reports decreased urine output. Vital signs show: Temperature 37.5°C, Heart Rate 140 bpm, Respiratory Rate 28 breaths/min, Blood Pressure 90/60 mmHg. Physical examination reveals pallor, multiple petechiae, and mild periorbital edema. Laboratory results are significant for: Hemoglobin 6.5 g/dL (reference range 11.5-15.5 g/dL), Platelets 25,000/uL (reference range 150,000-450,000/uL), Serum Creatinine 3.8 mg/dL (reference range 0.3-0.7 mg/dL), BUN 45 mg/dL (reference range 7-20 mg/dL), and a peripheral blood smear showing numerous schistocytes (fragmented red blood cells). Stool culture confirms the presence of E. coli O157:H7. Urinalysis shows 3+ blood and 1+ protein. Which cellular receptor, highly expressed on glomerular endothelial cells, is the primary target for Shiga toxin binding, initiating the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) CD40 receptor
(B) Mannose receptor
(C) Globotriaosylceramide (Gb3) receptor
(D) Toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4)
(E) Programmed cell death protein 1 (PD-1) receptor

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Exposure:** 4-year-old child, undercooked hamburger -> High suspicion for E. coli O157:H7 infection.
- **Initial Symptoms:** Bloody diarrhea -> Consistent with enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC) infection.
- **Subsequent Symptoms:** Pallor, petechiae, oliguria, lethargy -> Suggestive of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA), thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury (AKI).
- **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia, hypotension -> May indicate volume depletion or sepsis.
- **Physical Exam:** Pallor (anemia), petechiae (thrombocytopenia), edema (AKI) -> Consistent with the classic triad of HUS.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Low Hemoglobin (6.5 g/dL) + Schistocytes on smear -> MAHA (red blood cells fragmented as they pass through microthrombi).
    - Low Platelets (25,000/uL) -> Thrombocytopenia (platelets consumed in microthrombi).
    - High Creatinine (3.8 mg/dL) and BUN (45 mg/dL) -> AKI (impaired glomerular filtration).
    - Positive stool culture for E. coli O157:H7 -> Confirms the causative agent.
    - Urinalysis: Hematuria and proteinuria -> Glomerular damage.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific cellular receptor on glomerular endothelial cells that binds Shiga toxin.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Pathogenesis of STEC-HUS:** E. coli O157:H7 produces Shiga toxin (Stx), also known as verotoxin. Stx consists of two subunits, A and B. The B subunit pentamer binds to specific host cell surface receptors, facilitating the entry of the A subunit. The A subunit is an N-glycosidase that cleaves a specific adenine residue from the 28S ribosomal RNA of eukaryotic cells, thereby inhibiting protein synthesis and leading to cell death (apoptosis or necrosis).
- **Target Cells:** While Stx can affect various cell types, its primary targets in HUS pathogenesis are renal glomerular endothelial cells and intestinal epithelial cells. The high expression of the specific receptor on these cells explains their vulnerability.
- **Receptor Identification:** The specific receptor for Shiga toxin is Globotriaosylceramide (Gb3), also known as CD77. Gb3 is a glycosphingolipid found on the surface of many cell types, but it is particularly abundant on renal glomerular endothelial cells, intestinal epithelial cells, and neurons.
- **Mechanism in HUS:** Binding of Stx to Gb3 on glomerular endothelial cells triggers endothelial cell injury, swelling, and detachment from the glomerular basement membrane (GBM). This endothelial denudation exposes the underlying thrombogenic subendothelial matrix (collagen, von Willebrand factor). This exposure initiates platelet adhesion, aggregation, and the formation of microthrombi within the glomerular capillaries. These microthrombi occlude the capillaries, leading to decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR), ischemia, and AKI. The passage of red blood cells through these partially occluded capillaries causes mechanical shearing, resulting in the formation of schistocytes (fragmented RBCs), characteristic of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA). Platelets are also consumed in the formation of these microthrombi, leading to thrombocytopenia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **CD40 receptor:** This is a costimulatory protein found on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like B cells, macrophages, and dendritic cells. It binds to CD40 ligand (CD40L) on T cells and is crucial for T cell-dependent B cell activation, isotype switching, and germinal center formation. It is not involved in Shiga toxin binding or HUS pathogenesis.
- (B) **Mannose receptor:** This is a C-type lectin receptor found primarily on macrophages and dendritic cells. It recognizes mannose and fucose residues on microbial surfaces and is involved in phagocytosis and antigen presentation. It is not the receptor for Shiga toxin.
- (D) **Toll-like receptor 4 (TLR4):** This is a pattern recognition receptor (PRR) primarily expressed on immune cells (macrophages, dendritic cells) and some epithelial cells. It recognizes lipopolysaccharide (LPS), a component of the outer membrane of Gram-negative bacteria. TLR4 activation triggers inflammatory responses. While E. coli O157:H7 is Gram-negative, TLR4 is not the receptor for Shiga toxin itself, although LPS might contribute to the inflammatory milieu.
- (E) **Programmed cell death protein 1 (PD-1) receptor:** This is an inhibitory receptor expressed on T cells, B cells, and NK cells. It binds to PD-L1 and PD-L2 ligands expressed on various cells, including antigen-presenting cells and tumor cells, and regulates immune responses, preventing autoimmunity and maintaining immune homeostasis. It has no role in Shiga toxin binding or HUS pathogenesis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is that Shiga toxin binds to the Gb3 receptor on glomerular endothelial cells to initiate the pathogenesis of STEC-HUS. This understanding is crucial for recognizing the pathophysiology of this condition. Differential diagnoses for thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) include Thrombotic Thrombocytopenic Purpura (TTP), which involves deficiency of ADAMTS13 leading to uncleaved von Willebrand factor multimers, and atypical HUS (aHUS), which involves complement dysregulation. However, STEC-HUS is specifically linked to Shiga toxin binding to Gb3.

<a id='question-080'></a>

### Question 080: Atypical Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Atypical Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS) is caused by dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway, leading to endothelial cell injury and microvascular thrombosis, particularly in the kidney. (非典型溶血尿毒综合征 (aHUS) 是由替代补体通路的失调引起的，导致内皮细胞损伤和微血管血栓形成，尤其是在肾脏。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 24-year-old female presents to the emergency department with acute onset of fatigue, dyspnea, and lower extremity edema. Her past medical history is unremarkable except for a recent upper respiratory infection three weeks prior. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 160/100 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, and temperature of 37.2°C. Physical examination reveals pallor, petechiae on the lower extremities, and mild jaundice. Laboratory investigations reveal: Serum creatinine 4.5 mg/dL (normal <1.2 mg/dL), BUN 45 mg/dL (normal 7-20 mg/dL), Hemoglobin 7.5 g/dL (normal 12-16 g/dL), Platelet count 45,000/µL (normal 150,000-400,000/µL), LDH 1200 U/L (normal <250 U/L), Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (normal 30-200 mg/dL), Peripheral blood smear showing schistocytes, Urinalysis showing 3+ blood and 1+ protein, Microalbuminuria 1500 mg/g creatinine (normal <30 mg/g creatinine), and negative stool culture for Shiga toxin. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous loss-of-function mutation in the CFH gene. The patient is diagnosed with atypical Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS) and is started on eculizumab. Which complement pathway is primarily responsible for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) The classical complement pathway
(B) The lectin complement pathway
(C) The alternative complement pathway
(D) The coagulation cascade
(E) The contact activation system

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young female with acute renal failure (Cr 4.5), microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (low Hb, high LDH, low haptoglobin, schistocytes), and thrombocytopenia (low platelets). This triad is characteristic of Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (HUS).
- **Absence of Diarrhea & Negative Stool Culture:** Rules out typical (Shiga toxin-mediated) HUS, which is usually preceded by E. coli O157:H7 infection.
- **Genetic Mutation in CFH:** CFH encodes Complement Factor H, a key regulator of the alternative complement pathway. Loss-of-function mutations in CFH are a common cause of aHUS.
- **Treatment with Eculizumab:** Eculizumab is a monoclonal antibody that inhibits C5 cleavage, blocking the formation of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) and C5a. It is the standard treatment for aHUS, confirming the role of the terminal complement pathway, which is activated downstream of all three initial pathways (classical, lectin, alternative).
- **Pathophysiology Clues:** The combination of microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury points towards microvascular thrombosis. Unregulated complement activation leads to endothelial cell damage and platelet activation, causing these thrombotic microangiopathies.
- **Conclusion:** The clinical picture (aHUS without diarrhea), genetic finding (CFH mutation), and treatment (eculizumab) strongly implicate dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway.

##### 2. Physiological & Molecular Deep-Dive (生理机制、微循环动力学与神经体液调节深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of the innate immune system, involving a cascade of proteins that leads to opsonization, inflammation, and cell lysis. There are three main activation pathways: classical, lectin, and alternative.
- **Classical Pathway:** Initiated by antigen-antibody complexes binding to C1q, leading to activation of C1r and C1s, which cleave C4 and C2.
- **Lectin Pathway:** Initiated by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) or ficolins binding to microbial surfaces, activating MBL-associated serine proteases (MASPs), which cleave C4 and C2.
- **Alternative Pathway:** Constitutively active at low levels, providing immune surveillance. It is initiated by spontaneous hydrolysis of C3 (C3(H2O)) or by C3b deposited on microbial surfaces. Factor B binds to C3b, and Factor D cleaves Factor B into Ba and Bb. C3bBb forms the C3 convertase (C3bBb). This convertase cleaves more C3 into C3a and C3b, amplifying the cascade.
- **Regulation of Alternative Pathway:** The alternative pathway is tightly regulated to prevent excessive activation on host cells. Key regulators include:
    - **Factor H:** Binds to C3b and acts as a cofactor for Factor I, which cleaves C3b into inactive iC3b. It also competes with Factor B for binding to C3b. Mutations in CFH (as in this patient) lead to reduced Factor H activity, resulting in uncontrolled C3b deposition.
    - **Factor I:** A serine protease that cleaves C3b and C4b.
    - **Membrane Cofactor Protein (MCP / CD46):** Acts as a cofactor for Factor I in cleaving C3b and C4b on host cell surfaces.
    - **CD55 (DAF):** Decelerates the assembly of C3 convertases (C3bBb and C4b2a) on host cell surfaces.
    - **CD59 (MIRL):** Inhibits the formation of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) on host cell surfaces.
- **Pathogenesis of aHUS:** In aHUS caused by mutations in complement regulators (like CFH, CFI, MCP), the alternative pathway is chronically overactive. Uncontrolled C3b deposition occurs on endothelial cell surfaces, particularly in the glomeruli and small vessels. This leads to:
    - Assembly of C3 convertases (C3bBb) on endothelial surfaces.
    - Amplified C3 cleavage, generating C3a (anaphylatoxin) and C3b.
    - Formation of C5 convertases (C3bBbC3b).
    - Cleavage of C5 into C5a (anaphylatoxin and chemoattractant) and C5b.
    - Assembly of the Membrane Attack Complex (MAC, C5b-9) on endothelial cells, causing direct cell lysis and damage.
    - Endothelial cell activation, inflammation, and recruitment of inflammatory cells.
    - Platelet activation and aggregation, leading to microthrombi formation in small vessels.
    - Glomerular damage (thrombotic microangiopathy), causing acute kidney injury, proteinuria, and hematuria.
- **Role of Eculizumab:** Eculizumab binds specifically to C5, preventing its cleavage into C5a and C5b. This blocks the formation of the MAC and reduces C5a-mediated inflammation, effectively halting the downstream effects of complement activation and resolving the microvascular thrombosis in aHUS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The classical complement pathway: This pathway is typically initiated by immune complexes (antigen-antibody). While it can contribute to inflammation, it is not the primary pathway dysregulated in aHUS caused by mutations in CFH, CFI, or MCP. These mutations specifically affect the alternative pathway's regulation.
- (B) The lectin complement pathway: This pathway is initiated by the binding of mannose-binding lectin (MBL) or ficolins to carbohydrates on microbial surfaces. It is primarily involved in recognizing pathogens and is not the main pathway implicated in aHUS pathogenesis related to genetic defects in complement regulators.
- (D) The coagulation cascade: This system involves a series of clotting factors leading to fibrin formation and clot stabilization. While microthrombi are a feature of aHUS, the primary initiating event is complement-mediated endothelial damage, not a primary defect in the coagulation cascade itself (though complement activation can influence coagulation).
- (E) The contact activation system: This system (intrinsic pathway of coagulation) is initiated by contact with negatively charged surfaces and involves factors like Factor XII, prekallikrein, and high-molecular-weight kininogen. It plays a role in inflammation and coagulation but is not the primary pathway responsible for the endothelial injury seen in aHUS due to complement regulator mutations.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Atypical HUS (aHUS) is a thrombotic microangiopathy caused by uncontrolled activation of the ALTERNATIVE complement pathway due to genetic defects in its regulators (e.g., CFH, CFI, MCP) or gain-of-function mutations in C3/Factor B. This leads to endothelial cell damage, microthrombi formation, and organ failure, particularly affecting the kidneys. Treatment involves inhibiting the terminal complement pathway (e.g., eculizumab). Key differentials include typical (Shiga toxin-mediated) HUS and other causes of thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP).
